Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just can’t seem to sit still much anymore. Or maybe it’s not “anymore,” but it’s gotten more noticeable this past year or so. I’m always puttering around the house, fixing little things, rearranging drawers, signing up to help with one more thing when I probably should just sit down.

Even at 87, you’d think I’d be happy to rest, but I get antsy just sitting there. My daughter says, “Mother, just relax,” and I’ll say I am relaxing, but then I’m up straightening the magazines or checking the kitchen. It’s not anything dramatic, I guess, but it feels like I’m always in motion.

Repetitive thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d put it that way. It’s more that I notice something and then I’m up doing it before I’ve really decided to. Like, if a picture is crooked or the tea towels are in the wrong drawer—well, “wrong” according to me, I suppose—I just go fix it.

No, I don’t think so. Not “something bad,” exactly. It’s not like I’m afraid the house will fall down because a picture is crooked.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just can’t seem to sit still much anymore. I’m always puttering around the house, fixing little things, rearranging drawers, signing up to help with one more thing when I probably should just sit down.\n\nEven at 87, you’d think I’d be happy to rest, but I get antsy just sitting there. Maybe a little, but I don’t think of it as “control,” really. It’s more… if I’m up and doing something, I feel better than just sitting there noticing everything.

I’ll tell myself I’m just going to sit with my tea, and then I notice a plant, or a drawer, or remember someone at church needed a call—well, not always church, sometimes the library group—and off I go.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose it does make things a bit neater, yes, but I don’t think that’s really the main thing. I mean, if the tea towels are folded straight, well, that’s nice, isn’t it? My grandmother could fold linens so crisply you’d think they came from a hotel, and she never wasted a motion.

But for me lately—this past year or so—it’s more that I’m just always up doing something. Puttering, fixing a little thing here, arranging something there, agreeing to help with one more volunteer bit. And then I look up and realize I never actually sat down with my tea like I meant to.

So maybe the house ends up more orderly, but I think the bigger thing is I have a hard time being still, even at 87.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F42.2, G30.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just can’t seem to sit still much anymore. Or maybe it’s not “anymore,” but it’s gotten more noticeable this past year or so. I’m always puttering around the house, fixing little things, rearranging drawers, signing up to help with one more thing when I probably should just sit down.\n\nEven at 87, you’d think I’d be happy to rest, but I get antsy just sitting there. My daughter says, “Mother, just relax,” and I’ll say I am relaxing, but then I’m up straightening the magazines or checking the kitchen. It’s not anything dramatic, I guess, but it feels like I’m always in motion.\n\nDoctor: Does this constant need to be in motion cause you any distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, a bit. I mean, I try not to make a fuss about it, but I do feel badly afterward sometimes. At the senior center, for instance—oh, they have this little sign-up table, and everyone gets in a muddle about who’s bringing cookies and who’s driving whom, and I suppose I can get a little… brisk.\n\nI’ve had to apologize after meetings because I could tell I’d made people tense. One friend even suggested maybe I take a smaller role at the table, which stung a little, honestly. I don’t mean to upset anyone. I just get going and then it’s hard to soften myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you feel compelled to act on when you're puttering around or fixing things?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d put it that way. It’s more that I notice something and then I’m up doing it before I’ve really decided to. Like, if a picture is crooked or the tea towels are in the wrong drawer—well, “wrong” according to me, I suppose—I just go fix it.\n\nBut I don’t know that there’s a particular thought I’m having over and over, or that I feel I must do it or something terrible will happen. It’s more… I see it, I move. Then while I’m doing that, I remember the plant needs water, and then I’m in the kitchen, and—oh, I had a neighbor once who used to label every single shelf in her pantry, very tidy woman, but that’s not really me. I mean, if the tea towels are folded straight, well, that’s nice, isn’t it? My grandmother could fold linens so crisply you’d think they came from a hotel, and she never wasted a motion.\n\nBut for me lately—this past year or so—it’s more that I’m just always up doing something. Puttering, fixing a little thing here, arranging something there, agreeing to help with one more volunteer bit. And then I look up and realize I never actually sat down with my tea like I meant to.\n\nSo maybe the house ends up more orderly, but I think the bigger thing is I have a hard time being still, even at 87.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that you're particularly bothered by things being asymmetrical or not exactly as they should be?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m always puttering around the house, fixing little things, rearranging drawers, signing up to help with one more thing when I probably should just sit down.\n\nEven at 87, you’d think I’d be happy to rest, but I get antsy just sitting there. My daughter says, “Mother, just relax,” and I’ll say I am relaxing, but then I’m up straightening the magazines or checking the kitchen. It’s not anything dramatic, I guess, but it feels like I’m always in motion.\n\nDoctor: Does this constant need to be in motion cause you any distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, a bit. I mean, I try not to make a fuss about it, but I do feel badly afterward sometimes. At the senior center, for instance—oh, they have this little sign-up table, and everyone gets in a muddle about who’s bringing cookies and who’s driving whom, and I suppose I can get a little… brisk.\n\nI’ve had to apologize after meetings because I could tell I’d made people tense. One friend even suggested maybe I take a smaller role at the table, which stung a little, honestly. I don’t mean to upset anyone. I just get going and then it’s hard to soften myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you feel compelled to act on when you're puttering around or fixing things?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d put it that way. It’s more that I notice something and then I’m up doing it before I’ve really decided to. It’s more… I see it, I move. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just can’t seem to sit still much anymore. Or maybe it’s not “anymore,” but it’s gotten more noticeable this past year or so. I’m always puttering around the house, fixing little things, rearranging drawers, signing up to help with one more thing when I probably should just sit down.\n\nEven at 87, you’d think I’d be happy to rest, but I get antsy just sitting there. My daughter says, “Mother, just relax,” and I’ll say I am relaxing, but then I’m up straightening the magazines or checking the kitchen. It’s not anything dramatic, I guess, but it feels like I’m always in motion.\n\nDoctor: Does this constant need to be in motion cause you any distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, a bit. I mean, I try not to make a fuss about it, but I do feel badly afterward sometimes. At the senior center, for instance—oh, they have this little sign-up table, and everyone gets in a muddle about who’s bringing cookies and who’s driving whom, and I suppose I can get a little… brisk.\n\nI’ve had to apologize after meetings because I could tell I’d made people tense. One friend even suggested maybe I take a smaller role at the table, which stung a little, honestly. I don’t mean to upset anyone. I just get going and then it’s hard to soften myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you feel compelled to act on when you're puttering around or fixing things?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d put it that way. It’s more that I notice something and then I’m up doing it before I’ve really decided to. Like, if a picture is crooked or the tea towels are in the wrong drawer—well, “wrong” according to me, I suppose—I just go fix it.\n\nBut I don’t know that there’s a particular thought I’m having over and over, or that I feel I must do it or something terrible will happen. It’s more… I see it, I move. Then while I’m doing that, I remember the plant needs water, and then I’m in the kitchen, and—oh, I had a neighbor once who used to label every single shelf in her pantry, very tidy woman, but that’s not really me. I just get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't fix those things, like the picture or the tea towels?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not “something bad,” exactly. It’s not like I’m afraid the house will fall down because a picture is crooked.\n\nIt’s more that I see it and my hand is already reaching, if that makes sense. Like when my mother used to straighten the lace doilies—oh, she had doilies on everything, even the arms of the chairs, drove my father mad—but for me it’s just… I notice it, and then I’m fixing it before I’ve quite thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or just that they need to be done?\n\nPatient: Not perfectly, no… I wouldn’t say that. It’s not like I’m polishing the silver until I can see my face in it—though my aunt used to do that before company came, every Sunday practically.\n\nFor me it’s more that things need doing, and once I notice them I’m already up. That’s really part of why I came in, I suppose. This past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering—fixing this, arranging that, saying yes to one more little job or volunteer thing—and even at 87 I can’t seem to just sit down and be still for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things helps you feel more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think of it as “control,” really. It’s more… if I’m up and doing something, I feel better than just sitting there noticing everything.\n\nWhat brings me in is that for the past year or so, I seem to be constantly puttering—fixing this, arranging that, signing myself up to help with one more thing. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to sit with my tea, and then I notice a plant, or a drawer, or remember someone at church needed a call—well, not always church, sometimes the library group—and off I go.\n\nAt 87, you’d think I’d be better at sitting still, wouldn’t you? But I have a hard time just being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things is making your environment more orderly?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does make things a bit neater, yes, but I don’t think that’s really the main thing. I mean, if the tea towels are folded straight, well, that’s nice, isn’t it? My grandmother could fold linens so crisply you’d think they came from a hotel, and she never wasted a motion.\n\nBut for me lately—this past year or so—it’s more that I’m just always up doing something. Puttering, fixing a little thing here, arranging something there, agreeing to help with one more volunteer bit. And then I look up and realize I never actually sat down with my tea like I meant to.\n\nSo maybe the house ends up more orderly, but I think the bigger thing is I have a hard time being still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're particularly bothered by things being asymmetrical or not exactly as they should be?\n\nPatient: Asymmetrical… not especially, I don’t think. I’m not like that, no. If something catches my eye, I may fix it, but I wouldn’t say I’m particularly bothered by it being uneven or not perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities because they help keep your mind engaged?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does keep my mind busy, yes, though I don’t always think of it that way. It’s more like I notice one little thing and then another, and before I know it I’ve fixed the shelf, rearranged the papers, called someone about volunteering—oh, and then the kettle’s gone cold.\n\nThis past year or so, that’s been the pattern. I’m always puttering or arranging or signing myself up for one more thing. Even at 87, I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you're doing all these activities to avoid thinking about something else?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 87 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Constant_Activity\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that for the past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering, fixing, arranging, or volunteering for one more thing, and even at 87 I have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, no—not avoiding something in particular. I mean, I have the usual thoughts anyone has at my age, I suppose, but it’s not as if I’m trying not to think about one certain thing.

It’s more that I’m just always moving from one little task to the next. I’ll putter in the kitchen, then fix something that’s been crooked, then arrange a drawer, then say yes to another volunteer thing before I’ve even thought it through. My neighbor used to say, “Sit down before you wear a path in the floor,” and lately I think she’d be right.

So no, I wouldn’t say it’s avoidance exactly. What brings me in is more that for the past year or so, I can’t seem to just sit still, even at 87.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F42.2, G30.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just can’t seem to sit still much anymore. Or maybe it’s not “anymore,” but it’s gotten more noticeable this past year or so. I’m always puttering around the house, fixing little things, rearranging drawers, signing up to help with one more thing when I probably should just sit down.\n\nEven at 87, you’d think I’d be happy to rest, but I get antsy just sitting there. My daughter says, “Mother, just relax,” and I’ll say I am relaxing, but then I’m up straightening the magazines or checking the kitchen. It’s not anything dramatic, I guess, but it feels like I’m always in motion.\n\nDoctor: Does this constant need to be in motion cause you any distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, a bit. I mean, I try not to make a fuss about it, but I do feel badly afterward sometimes. At the senior center, for instance—oh, they have this little sign-up table, and everyone gets in a muddle about who’s bringing cookies and who’s driving whom, and I suppose I can get a little… brisk.\n\nI’ve had to apologize after meetings because I could tell I’d made people tense. One friend even suggested maybe I take a smaller role at the table, which stung a little, honestly. I don’t mean to upset anyone. I just get going and then it’s hard to soften myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you feel compelled to act on when you're puttering around or fixing things?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d put it that way. It’s more that I notice something and then I’m up doing it before I’ve really decided to. Like, if a picture is crooked or the tea towels are in the wrong drawer—well, “wrong” according to me, I suppose—I just go fix it.\n\nBut I don’t know that there’s a particular thought I’m having over and over, or that I feel I must do it or something terrible will happen. It’s more… I see it, I move. Then while I’m doing that, I remember the plant needs water, and then I’m in the kitchen, and—oh, I had a neighbor once who used to label every single shelf in her pantry, very tidy woman, but that’s not really me. I just get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't fix those things, like the picture or the tea towels?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not “something bad,” exactly. It’s not like I’m afraid the house will fall down because a picture is crooked.\n\nIt’s more that I see it and my hand is already reaching, if that makes sense. Like when my mother used to straighten the lace doilies—oh, she had doilies on everything, even the arms of the chairs, drove my father mad—but for me it’s just… I notice it, and then I’m fixing it before I’ve quite thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or just that they need to be done?\n\nPatient: Not perfectly, no… I wouldn’t say that. It’s not like I’m polishing the silver until I can see my face in it—though my aunt used to do that before company came, every Sunday practically.\n\nFor me it’s more that things need doing, and once I notice them I’m already up. That’s really part of why I came in, I suppose. This past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering—fixing this, arranging that, saying yes to one more little job or volunteer thing—and even at 87 I can’t seem to just sit down and be still for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things helps you feel more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think of it as “control,” really. It’s more… if I’m up and doing something, I feel better than just sitting there noticing everything.\n\nWhat brings me in is that for the past year or so, I seem to be constantly puttering—fixing this, arranging that, signing myself up to help with one more thing. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to sit with my tea, and then I notice a plant, or a drawer, or remember someone at church needed a call—well, not always church, sometimes the library group—and off I go.\n\nAt 87, you’d think I’d be better at sitting still, wouldn’t you? But I have a hard time just being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things is making your environment more orderly?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does make things a bit neater, yes, but I don’t think that’s really the main thing. I mean, if the tea towels are folded straight, well, that’s nice, isn’t it? My grandmother could fold linens so crisply you’d think they came from a hotel, and she never wasted a motion.\n\nBut for me lately—this past year or so—it’s more that I’m just always up doing something. Puttering, fixing a little thing here, arranging something there, agreeing to help with one more volunteer bit. And then I look up and realize I never actually sat down with my tea like I meant to.\n\nSo maybe the house ends up more orderly, but I think the bigger thing is I have a hard time being still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're particularly bothered by things being asymmetrical or not exactly as they should be?\n\nPatient: Asymmetrical… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, if a picture is hanging crooked I might straighten it, but I don’t sit there feeling upset because it’s not exactly so.\n\nMy sister-in-law used to be very particular about things lining up on the mantel—little porcelain birds, all facing the same way—and I remember thinking, goodness, that seems like a lot to keep track of. I’m not like that, no. If something catches my eye, I may fix it, but I wouldn’t say I’m particularly bothered by it being uneven or not perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities because they help keep your mind engaged?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does keep my mind busy, yes, though I don’t always think of it that way. It’s more like I notice one little thing and then another, and before I know it I’ve fixed the shelf, rearranged the papers, called someone about volunteering—oh, and then the kettle’s gone cold.\n\nThis past year or so, that’s been the pattern. I’m always puttering or arranging or signing myself up for one more thing. Even at 87, I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all these activities to avoid thinking about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not avoiding something in particular. I mean, I have the usual thoughts anyone has at my age, I suppose, but it’s not as if I’m trying not to think about one certain thing.\n\nIt’s more that I’m just always moving from one little task to the next. I’ll putter in the kitchen, then fix something that’s been crooked, then arrange a drawer, then say yes to another volunteer thing before I’ve even thought it through. I’ll putter in the kitchen, then fix something that’s been crooked, then arrange a drawer, then say yes to another volunteer thing before I’ve even thought it through. My neighbor used to say, “Sit down before you wear a path in the floor,” and lately I think she’d be right.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s avoidance exactly. What brings me in is more that for the past year or so, I can’t seem to just sit still, even at 87.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're doing these activities mainly because they help keep your mind active?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 87 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Constant_Activity\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that for the past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering, fixing, arranging, or volunteering for one more thing, and even at 87 I have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe partly, but I wouldn’t say “mainly” in a deliberate way. It’s not as if I wake up and think, I must keep my mind active today.

It’s more that I just seem to keep going—puttering around, fixing little things, arranging this or that, and then someone asks about a volunteer job and I say yes before I’ve really sat with it. My mother was like that too, always had a dish towel in her hand, though she at least seemed to finish one thing before starting another.

But yes, what brings me in is that for the past year or so, I’ve had such a hard time just sitting down and being still, even at 87.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just can’t seem to sit still much anymore. Or maybe it’s not “anymore,” but it’s gotten more noticeable this past year or so. I’m always puttering around the house, fixing little things, rearranging drawers, signing up to help with one more thing when I probably should just sit down.\n\nEven at 87, you’d think I’d be happy to rest, but I get antsy just sitting there. My daughter says, “Mother, just relax,” and I’ll say I am relaxing, but then I’m up straightening the magazines or checking the kitchen. It’s not anything dramatic, I guess, but it feels like I’m always in motion.\n\nDoctor: Does this constant need to be in motion cause you any distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, a bit. I mean, I try not to make a fuss about it, but I do feel badly afterward sometimes. At the senior center, for instance—oh, they have this little sign-up table, and everyone gets in a muddle about who’s bringing cookies and who’s driving whom, and I suppose I can get a little… brisk.\n\nI’ve had to apologize after meetings because I could tell I’d made people tense. One friend even suggested maybe I take a smaller role at the table, which stung a little, honestly. I don’t mean to upset anyone. I just get going and then it’s hard to soften myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you feel compelled to act on when you're puttering around or fixing things?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d put it that way. It’s more that I notice something and then I’m up doing it before I’ve really decided to. Like, if a picture is crooked or the tea towels are in the wrong drawer—well, “wrong” according to me, I suppose—I just go fix it.\n\nBut I don’t know that there’s a particular thought I’m having over and over, or that I feel I must do it or something terrible will happen. It’s more… I see it, I move. Then while I’m doing that, I remember the plant needs water, and then I’m in the kitchen, and—oh, I had a neighbor once who used to label every single shelf in her pantry, very tidy woman, but that’s not really me. I just get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't fix those things, like the picture or the tea towels?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not “something bad,” exactly. It’s not like I’m afraid the house will fall down because a picture is crooked.\n\nIt’s more that I see it and my hand is already reaching, if that makes sense. Like when my mother used to straighten the lace doilies—oh, she had doilies on everything, even the arms of the chairs, drove my father mad—but for me it’s just… I notice it, and then I’m fixing it before I’ve quite thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or just that they need to be done?\n\nPatient: Not perfectly, no… I wouldn’t say that. It’s not like I’m polishing the silver until I can see my face in it—though my aunt used to do that before company came, every Sunday practically.\n\nFor me it’s more that things need doing, and once I notice them I’m already up. That’s really part of why I came in, I suppose. This past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering—fixing this, arranging that, saying yes to one more little job or volunteer thing—and even at 87 I can’t seem to just sit down and be still for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things helps you feel more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think of it as “control,” really. It’s more… if I’m up and doing something, I feel better than just sitting there noticing everything.\n\nWhat brings me in is that for the past year or so, I seem to be constantly puttering—fixing this, arranging that, signing myself up to help with one more thing. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to sit with my tea, and then I notice a plant, or a drawer, or remember someone at church needed a call—well, not always church, sometimes the library group—and off I go.\n\nAt 87, you’d think I’d be better at sitting still, wouldn’t you? But I have a hard time just being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things is making your environment more orderly?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does make things a bit neater, yes, but I don’t think that’s really the main thing. I mean, if the tea towels are folded straight, well, that’s nice, isn’t it? My grandmother could fold linens so crisply you’d think they came from a hotel, and she never wasted a motion.\n\nBut for me lately—this past year or so—it’s more that I’m just always up doing something. Puttering, fixing a little thing here, arranging something there, agreeing to help with one more volunteer bit. And then I look up and realize I never actually sat down with my tea like I meant to.\n\nSo maybe the house ends up more orderly, but I think the bigger thing is I have a hard time being still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're particularly bothered by things being asymmetrical or not exactly as they should be?\n\nPatient: Asymmetrical… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, if a picture is hanging crooked I might straighten it, but I don’t sit there feeling upset because it’s not exactly so.\n\nMy sister-in-law used to be very particular about things lining up on the mantel—little porcelain birds, all facing the same way—and I remember thinking, goodness, that seems like a lot to keep track of. I’m not like that, no. If something catches my eye, I may fix it, but I wouldn’t say I’m particularly bothered by it being uneven or not perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities because they help keep your mind engaged?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does keep my mind busy, yes, though I don’t always think of it that way. It’s more like I notice one little thing and then another, and before I know it I’ve fixed the shelf, rearranged the papers, called someone about volunteering—oh, and then the kettle’s gone cold.\n\nThis past year or so, that’s been the pattern. I’m always puttering or arranging or signing myself up for one more thing. Even at 87, I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all these activities to avoid thinking about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not avoiding something in particular. I mean, I have the usual thoughts anyone has at my age, I suppose, but it’s not as if I’m trying not to think about one certain thing.\n\nIt’s more that I’m just always moving from one little task to the next. What brings me in is more that for the past year or so, I can’t seem to just sit still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities mainly because they help keep your mind active?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but I wouldn’t say “mainly” in a deliberate way. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities because they help keep your mind engaged?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does keep my mind busy, yes, though I don’t always think of it that way. It’s more like I notice one little thing and then another, and before I know it I’ve fixed the shelf, rearranged the papers, called someone about volunteering—oh, and then the kettle’s gone cold.\n\nThis past year or so, that’s been the pattern. I’m always puttering or arranging or signing myself up for one more thing. Even at 87, I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all these activities to avoid thinking about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not avoiding something in particular. I mean, I have the usual thoughts anyone has at my age, I suppose, but it’s not as if I’m trying not to think about one certain thing.\n\nIt’s more that I’m just always moving from one little task to the next. I’ll putter in the kitchen, then fix something that’s been crooked, then arrange a drawer, then say yes to another volunteer thing before I’ve even thought it through. My neighbor used to say, “Sit down before you wear a path in the floor,” and lately I think she’d be right.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s avoidance exactly. What brings me in is more that for the past year or so, I can’t seem to just sit still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities mainly because they help keep your mind active?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but I wouldn’t say “mainly” in a deliberate way. It’s not as if I wake up and think, I must keep my mind active today.\n\nIt’s more that I just seem to keep going—puttering around, fixing little things, arranging this or that, and then someone asks about a volunteer job and I say yes before I’ve really sat with it. My mother was like that too, always had a dish towel in her hand, though she at least seemed to finish one thing before starting another.\n\nBut yes, what brings me in is that for the past year or so, I’ve had such a hard time just sitting down and being still, even at 87.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would feel bored or unfulfilled if you weren't constantly doing these activities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 87 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Constant_Activity\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that for the past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering, fixing, arranging, or volunteering for one more thing, and even at 87 I have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose I might feel a bit restless, more than bored exactly. “Unfulfilled” sounds a little grand for it, doesn’t it? I’m not climbing mountains—I’m straightening the pantry or fixing a loose handle or saying yes to help with some little volunteer thing.

But yes, if I’m not doing something, I do seem to get up and find something to do. I’ll sit down with tea, and then notice the cushions are crooked, and then I’m in the other room sorting something. My niece says I’m like one of those wind-up toys—she had one as a child, a little tin chicken, I think.

What brings me in is that for the past year or so I just feel like I’m always puttering, fixing, arranging, or taking on one more thing, and even at 87 I have a hard time simply sitting still.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F42.2, G30.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just can’t seem to sit still much anymore. Or maybe it’s not “anymore,” but it’s gotten more noticeable this past year or so. I’m always puttering around the house, fixing little things, rearranging drawers, signing up to help with one more thing when I probably should just sit down.\n\nEven at 87, you’d think I’d be happy to rest, but I get antsy just sitting there. My daughter says, “Mother, just relax,” and I’ll say I am relaxing, but then I’m up straightening the magazines or checking the kitchen. It’s not anything dramatic, I guess, but it feels like I’m always in motion.\n\nDoctor: Does this constant need to be in motion cause you any distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, a bit. I mean, I try not to make a fuss about it, but I do feel badly afterward sometimes. At the senior center, for instance—oh, they have this little sign-up table, and everyone gets in a muddle about who’s bringing cookies and who’s driving whom, and I suppose I can get a little… brisk.\n\nI’ve had to apologize after meetings because I could tell I’d made people tense. One friend even suggested maybe I take a smaller role at the table, which stung a little, honestly. I don’t mean to upset anyone. I just get going and then it’s hard to soften myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you feel compelled to act on when you're puttering around or fixing things?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d put it that way. It’s more that I notice something and then I’m up doing it before I’ve really decided to. Like, if a picture is crooked or the tea towels are in the wrong drawer—well, “wrong” according to me, I suppose—I just go fix it.\n\nBut I don’t know that there’s a particular thought I’m having over and over, or that I feel I must do it or something terrible will happen. It’s more… I see it, I move. Then while I’m doing that, I remember the plant needs water, and then I’m in the kitchen, and—oh, I had a neighbor once who used to label every single shelf in her pantry, very tidy woman, but that’s not really me. I just get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't fix those things, like the picture or the tea towels?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not “something bad,” exactly. It’s not like I’m afraid the house will fall down because a picture is crooked.\n\nIt’s more that I see it and my hand is already reaching, if that makes sense. Like when my mother used to straighten the lace doilies—oh, she had doilies on everything, even the arms of the chairs, drove my father mad—but for me it’s just… I notice it, and then I’m fixing it before I’ve quite thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or just that they need to be done?\n\nPatient: Not perfectly, no… I wouldn’t say that. It’s not like I’m polishing the silver until I can see my face in it—though my aunt used to do that before company came, every Sunday practically.\n\nFor me it’s more that things need doing, and once I notice them I’m already up. That’s really part of why I came in, I suppose. This past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering—fixing this, arranging that, saying yes to one more little job or volunteer thing—and even at 87 I can’t seem to just sit down and be still for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things helps you feel more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think of it as “control,” really. It’s more… if I’m up and doing something, I feel better than just sitting there noticing everything.\n\nWhat brings me in is that for the past year or so, I seem to be constantly puttering—fixing this, arranging that, signing myself up to help with one more thing. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to sit with my tea, and then I notice a plant, or a drawer, or remember someone at church needed a call—well, not always church, sometimes the library group—and off I go.\n\nAt 87, you’d think I’d be better at sitting still, wouldn’t you? But I have a hard time just being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things is making your environment more orderly?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does make things a bit neater, yes, but I don’t think that’s really the main thing. I mean, if the tea towels are folded straight, well, that’s nice, isn’t it? My grandmother could fold linens so crisply you’d think they came from a hotel, and she never wasted a motion.\n\nBut for me lately—this past year or so—it’s more that I’m just always up doing something. Puttering, fixing a little thing here, arranging something there, agreeing to help with one more volunteer bit. And then I look up and realize I never actually sat down with my tea like I meant to.\n\nSo maybe the house ends up more orderly, but I think the bigger thing is I have a hard time being still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're particularly bothered by things being asymmetrical or not exactly as they should be?\n\nPatient: Asymmetrical… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, if a picture is hanging crooked I might straighten it, but I don’t sit there feeling upset because it’s not exactly so.\n\nMy sister-in-law used to be very particular about things lining up on the mantel—little porcelain birds, all facing the same way—and I remember thinking, goodness, that seems like a lot to keep track of. I’m not like that, no. If something catches my eye, I may fix it, but I wouldn’t say I’m particularly bothered by it being uneven or not perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities because they help keep your mind engaged?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does keep my mind busy, yes, though I don’t always think of it that way. It’s more like I notice one little thing and then another, and before I know it I’ve fixed the shelf, rearranged the papers, called someone about volunteering—oh, and then the kettle’s gone cold.\n\nThis past year or so, that’s been the pattern. I’m always puttering or arranging or signing myself up for one more thing. Even at 87, I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all these activities to avoid thinking about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not avoiding something in particular. I mean, I have the usual thoughts anyone has at my age, I suppose, but it’s not as if I’m trying not to think about one certain thing.\n\nIt’s more that I’m just always moving from one little task to the next. I’ll putter in the kitchen, then fix something that’s been crooked, then arrange a drawer, then say yes to another volunteer thing before I’ve even thought it through. My neighbor used to say, “Sit down before you wear a path in the floor,” and lately I think she’d be right.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s avoidance exactly. What brings me in is more that for the past year or so, I can’t seem to just sit still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities mainly because they help keep your mind active?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but I wouldn’t say “mainly” in a deliberate way. It’s not as if I wake up and think, I must keep my mind active today.\n\nIt’s more that I just seem to keep going—puttering around, fixing little things, arranging this or that, and then someone asks about a volunteer job and I say yes before I’ve really sat with it. What brings me in is more that for the past year or so, I can’t seem to just sit still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities mainly because they help keep your mind active?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but I wouldn’t say “mainly” in a deliberate way. It’s not as if I wake up and think, I must keep my mind active today.\n\nIt’s more that I just seem to keep going—puttering around, fixing little things, arranging this or that, and then someone asks about a volunteer job and I say yes before I’ve really sat with it. My mother was like that too, always had a dish towel in her hand, though she at least seemed to finish one thing before starting another.\n\nBut yes, what brings me in is that for the past year or so, I’ve had such a hard time just sitting down and being still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel bored or unfulfilled if you weren't constantly doing these activities?\n\nPatient: I suppose I might feel a bit restless, more than bored exactly. “Unfulfilled” sounds a little grand for it, doesn’t it? I’m not climbing mountains—I’m straightening the pantry or fixing a loose handle or saying yes to help with some little volunteer thing.\n\nBut yes, if I’m not doing something, I do seem to get up and find something to do. I’ll sit down with tea, and then notice the cushions are crooked, and then I’m in the other room sorting something. My niece says I’m like one of those wind-up toys—she had one as a child, a little tin chicken, I think.\n\nWhat brings me in is that for the past year or so I just feel like I’m always puttering, fixing, arranging, or taking on one more thing, and even at 87 I have a hard time simply sitting still.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're doing these activities because you would otherwise feel mentally unstimulated?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 13\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d put it as “mentally unstimulated,” exactly. That sounds like I’m doing a crossword to keep the brain sharp—my sister used to do those in ink, which always seemed very confident to me.

For me it’s more… I just don’t seem to stop. What brings me in is that for the past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering, fixing something, arranging things, or volunteering for one more thing, and even at 87 I have a hard time just sitting down and being still. So maybe it’s stimulation in a way, but it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it for that reason.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F42.2, G30.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just can’t seem to sit still much anymore. Or maybe it’s not “anymore,” but it’s gotten more noticeable this past year or so. I’m always puttering around the house, fixing little things, rearranging drawers, signing up to help with one more thing when I probably should just sit down.\n\nEven at 87, you’d think I’d be happy to rest, but I get antsy just sitting there. My daughter says, “Mother, just relax,” and I’ll say I am relaxing, but then I’m up straightening the magazines or checking the kitchen. It’s not anything dramatic, I guess, but it feels like I’m always in motion.\n\nDoctor: Does this constant need to be in motion cause you any distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, a bit. I mean, I try not to make a fuss about it, but I do feel badly afterward sometimes. At the senior center, for instance—oh, they have this little sign-up table, and everyone gets in a muddle about who’s bringing cookies and who’s driving whom, and I suppose I can get a little… brisk.\n\nI’ve had to apologize after meetings because I could tell I’d made people tense. One friend even suggested maybe I take a smaller role at the table, which stung a little, honestly. I don’t mean to upset anyone. I just get going and then it’s hard to soften myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you feel compelled to act on when you're puttering around or fixing things?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d put it that way. It’s more that I notice something and then I’m up doing it before I’ve really decided to. Like, if a picture is crooked or the tea towels are in the wrong drawer—well, “wrong” according to me, I suppose—I just go fix it.\n\nBut I don’t know that there’s a particular thought I’m having over and over, or that I feel I must do it or something terrible will happen. It’s more… I see it, I move. Then while I’m doing that, I remember the plant needs water, and then I’m in the kitchen, and—oh, I had a neighbor once who used to label every single shelf in her pantry, very tidy woman, but that’s not really me. I just get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't fix those things, like the picture or the tea towels?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not “something bad,” exactly. It’s not like I’m afraid the house will fall down because a picture is crooked.\n\nIt’s more that I see it and my hand is already reaching, if that makes sense. Like when my mother used to straighten the lace doilies—oh, she had doilies on everything, even the arms of the chairs, drove my father mad—but for me it’s just… I notice it, and then I’m fixing it before I’ve quite thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or just that they need to be done?\n\nPatient: Not perfectly, no… I wouldn’t say that. It’s not like I’m polishing the silver until I can see my face in it—though my aunt used to do that before company came, every Sunday practically.\n\nFor me it’s more that things need doing, and once I notice them I’m already up. That’s really part of why I came in, I suppose. This past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering—fixing this, arranging that, saying yes to one more little job or volunteer thing—and even at 87 I can’t seem to just sit down and be still for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things helps you feel more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think of it as “control,” really. It’s more… if I’m up and doing something, I feel better than just sitting there noticing everything.\n\nWhat brings me in is that for the past year or so, I seem to be constantly puttering—fixing this, arranging that, signing myself up to help with one more thing. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to sit with my tea, and then I notice a plant, or a drawer, or remember someone at church needed a call—well, not always church, sometimes the library group—and off I go.\n\nAt 87, you’d think I’d be better at sitting still, wouldn’t you? But I have a hard time just being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things is making your environment more orderly?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does make things a bit neater, yes, but I don’t think that’s really the main thing. I mean, if the tea towels are folded straight, well, that’s nice, isn’t it? My grandmother could fold linens so crisply you’d think they came from a hotel, and she never wasted a motion.\n\nBut for me lately—this past year or so—it’s more that I’m just always up doing something. Puttering, fixing a little thing here, arranging something there, agreeing to help with one more volunteer bit. And then I look up and realize I never actually sat down with my tea like I meant to.\n\nSo maybe the house ends up more orderly, but I think the bigger thing is I have a hard time being still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're particularly bothered by things being asymmetrical or not exactly as they should be?\n\nPatient: Asymmetrical… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, if a picture is hanging crooked I might straighten it, but I don’t sit there feeling upset because it’s not exactly so.\n\nMy sister-in-law used to be very particular about things lining up on the mantel—little porcelain birds, all facing the same way—and I remember thinking, goodness, that seems like a lot to keep track of. I’m not like that, no. If something catches my eye, I may fix it, but I wouldn’t say I’m particularly bothered by it being uneven or not perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities because they help keep your mind engaged?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does keep my mind busy, yes, though I don’t always think of it that way. It’s more like I notice one little thing and then another, and before I know it I’ve fixed the shelf, rearranged the papers, called someone about volunteering—oh, and then the kettle’s gone cold.\n\nThis past year or so, that’s been the pattern. I’m always puttering or arranging or signing myself up for one more thing. Even at 87, I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all these activities to avoid thinking about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not avoiding something in particular. I mean, I have the usual thoughts anyone has at my age, I suppose, but it’s not as if I’m trying not to think about one certain thing.\n\nIt’s more that I’m just always moving from one little task to the next. I’ll putter in the kitchen, then fix something that’s been crooked, then arrange a drawer, then say yes to another volunteer thing before I’ve even thought it through. My neighbor used to say, “Sit down before you wear a path in the floor,” and lately I think she’d be right.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s avoidance exactly. What brings me in is more that for the past year or so, I can’t seem to just sit still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities mainly because they help keep your mind active?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but I wouldn’t say “mainly” in a deliberate way. It’s not as if I wake up and think, I must keep my mind active today.\n\nIt’s more that I just seem to keep going—puttering around, fixing little things, arranging this or that, and then someone asks about a volunteer job and I say yes before I’ve really sat with it. My mother was like that too, always had a dish towel in her hand, though she at least seemed to finish one thing before starting another.\n\nBut yes, what brings me in is that for the past year or so, I’ve had such a hard time just sitting down and being still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel bored or unfulfilled if you weren't constantly doing these activities?\n\nPatient: I suppose I might feel a bit restless, more than bored exactly. “Unfulfilled” sounds a little grand for it, doesn’t it? I’m not climbing mountains—I’m straightening the pantry or fixing a loose handle or saying yes to help with some little volunteer thing.\n\nBut yes, if I’m not doing something, I do seem to get up and find something to do. I’ll sit down with tea, and then notice the cushions are crooked, and then I’m in the other room sorting something. My niece says I’m like one of those wind-up toys—she had one as a child, a little tin chicken, I think.\n\nWhat brings me in is that for the past year or so I just feel like I’m always puttering, fixing, arranging, or taking on one more thing, and even at 87 I have a hard time simply sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities because you would otherwise feel mentally unstimulated?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it as “mentally unstimulated,” exactly. That sounds like I’m doing a crossword to keep the brain sharp—my sister used to do those in ink, which always seemed very confident to me.\n\nFor me it’s more… I just don’t seem to stop. What brings me in is that for the past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering, fixing something, arranging things, or volunteering for one more thing, and even at 87 I have a hard time just sitting down and being still. So maybe it’s stimulation in a way, but it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it for that reason.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just can’t seem to sit still much anymore. Or maybe it’s not “anymore,” but it’s gotten more noticeable this past year or so. I’m always puttering around the house, fixing little things, rearranging drawers, signing up to help with one more thing when I probably should just sit down.\n\nEven at 87, you’d think I’d be happy to rest, but I get antsy just sitting there. My daughter says, “Mother, just relax,” and I’ll say I am relaxing, but then I’m up straightening the magazines or checking the kitchen. It’s not anything dramatic, I guess, but it feels like I’m always in motion.\n\nDoctor: Does this constant need to be in motion cause you any distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, a bit. I mean, I try not to make a fuss about it, but I do feel badly afterward sometimes. At the senior center, for instance—oh, they have this little sign-up table, and everyone gets in a muddle about who’s bringing cookies and who’s driving whom, and I suppose I can get a little… brisk.\n\nI’ve had to apologize after meetings because I could tell I’d made people tense. One friend even suggested maybe I take a smaller role at the table, which stung a little, honestly. I don’t mean to upset anyone. I just get going and then it’s hard to soften myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you feel compelled to act on when you're puttering around or fixing things?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d put it that way. It’s more that I notice something and then I’m up doing it before I’ve really decided to. Like, if a picture is crooked or the tea towels are in the wrong drawer—well, “wrong” according to me, I suppose—I just go fix it.\n\nBut I don’t know that there’s a particular thought I’m having over and over, or that I feel I must do it or something terrible will happen. It’s more… I see it, I move. Then while I’m doing that, I remember the plant needs water, and then I’m in the kitchen, and—oh, I had a neighbor once who used to label every single shelf in her pantry, very tidy woman, but that’s not really me. I just get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't fix those things, like the picture or the tea towels?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not “something bad,” exactly. It’s not like I’m afraid the house will fall down because a picture is crooked.\n\nIt’s more that I see it and my hand is already reaching, if that makes sense. Like when my mother used to straighten the lace doilies—oh, she had doilies on everything, even the arms of the chairs, drove my father mad—but for me it’s just… I notice it, and then I’m fixing it before I’ve quite thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done perfectly, or just that they need to be done?\n\nPatient: Not perfectly, no… I wouldn’t say that. It’s not like I’m polishing the silver until I can see my face in it—though my aunt used to do that before company came, every Sunday practically.\n\nFor me it’s more that things need doing, and once I notice them I’m already up. That’s really part of why I came in, I suppose. This past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering—fixing this, arranging that, saying yes to one more little job or volunteer thing—and even at 87 I can’t seem to just sit down and be still for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things helps you feel more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think of it as “control,” really. It’s more… if I’m up and doing something, I feel better than just sitting there noticing everything.\n\nWhat brings me in is that for the past year or so, I seem to be constantly puttering—fixing this, arranging that, signing myself up to help with one more thing. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to sit with my tea, and then I notice a plant, or a drawer, or remember someone at church needed a call—well, not always church, sometimes the library group—and off I go.\n\nAt 87, you’d think I’d be better at sitting still, wouldn’t you? But I have a hard time just being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things is making your environment more orderly?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does make things a bit neater, yes, but I don’t think that’s really the main thing. I mean, if the tea towels are folded straight, well, that’s nice, isn’t it? My grandmother could fold linens so crisply you’d think they came from a hotel, and she never wasted a motion.\n\nBut for me lately—this past year or so—it’s more that I’m just always up doing something. Puttering, fixing a little thing here, arranging something there, agreeing to help with one more volunteer bit. And then I look up and realize I never actually sat down with my tea like I meant to.\n\nSo maybe the house ends up more orderly, but I think the bigger thing is I have a hard time being still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're particularly bothered by things being asymmetrical or not exactly as they should be?\n\nPatient: Asymmetrical… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, if a picture is hanging crooked I might straighten it, but I don’t sit there feeling upset because it’s not exactly so.\n\nMy sister-in-law used to be very particular about things lining up on the mantel—little porcelain birds, all facing the same way—and I remember thinking, goodness, that seems like a lot to keep track of. I’m not like that, no. If something catches my eye, I may fix it, but I wouldn’t say I’m particularly bothered by it being uneven or not perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities because they help keep your mind engaged?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does keep my mind busy, yes, though I don’t always think of it that way. It’s more like I notice one little thing and then another, and before I know it I’ve fixed the shelf, rearranged the papers, called someone about volunteering—oh, and then the kettle’s gone cold.\n\nThis past year or so, that’s been the pattern. I’m always puttering or arranging or signing myself up for one more thing. Even at 87, I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all these activities to avoid thinking about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not avoiding something in particular. I mean, I have the usual thoughts anyone has at my age, I suppose, but it’s not as if I’m trying not to think about one certain thing.\n\nIt’s more that I’m just always moving from one little task to the next. I’ll putter in the kitchen, then fix something that’s been crooked, then arrange a drawer, then say yes to another volunteer thing before I’ve even thought it through. My neighbor used to say, “Sit down before you wear a path in the floor,” and lately I think she’d be right.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s avoidance exactly. What brings me in is more that for the past year or so, I can’t seem to just sit still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities mainly because they help keep your mind active?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but I wouldn’t say “mainly” in a deliberate way. This past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering—fixing this, arranging that, saying yes to one more little job or volunteer thing—and even at 87 I can’t seem to just sit down and be still for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things helps you feel more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think of it as “control,” really. It’s more… if I’m up and doing something, I feel better than just sitting there noticing everything.\n\nWhat brings me in is that for the past year or so, I seem to be constantly puttering—fixing this, arranging that, signing myself up to help with one more thing. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to sit with my tea, and then I notice a plant, or a drawer, or remember someone at church needed a call—well, not always church, sometimes the library group—and off I go.\n\nAt 87, you’d think I’d be better at sitting still, wouldn’t you? But I have a hard time just being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your puttering and fixing things is making your environment more orderly?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does make things a bit neater, yes, but I don’t think that’s really the main thing. I mean, if the tea towels are folded straight, well, that’s nice, isn’t it? My grandmother could fold linens so crisply you’d think they came from a hotel, and she never wasted a motion.\n\nBut for me lately—this past year or so—it’s more that I’m just always up doing something. Puttering, fixing a little thing here, arranging something there, agreeing to help with one more volunteer bit. And then I look up and realize I never actually sat down with my tea like I meant to.\n\nSo maybe the house ends up more orderly, but I think the bigger thing is I have a hard time being still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're particularly bothered by things being asymmetrical or not exactly as they should be?\n\nPatient: Asymmetrical… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, if a picture is hanging crooked I might straighten it, but I don’t sit there feeling upset because it’s not exactly so.\n\nMy sister-in-law used to be very particular about things lining up on the mantel—little porcelain birds, all facing the same way—and I remember thinking, goodness, that seems like a lot to keep track of. I’m not like that, no. If something catches my eye, I may fix it, but I wouldn’t say I’m particularly bothered by it being uneven or not perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities because they help keep your mind engaged?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does keep my mind busy, yes, though I don’t always think of it that way. It’s more like I notice one little thing and then another, and before I know it I’ve fixed the shelf, rearranged the papers, called someone about volunteering—oh, and then the kettle’s gone cold.\n\nThis past year or so, that’s been the pattern. I’m always puttering or arranging or signing myself up for one more thing. Even at 87, I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all these activities to avoid thinking about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not avoiding something in particular. I mean, I have the usual thoughts anyone has at my age, I suppose, but it’s not as if I’m trying not to think about one certain thing.\n\nIt’s more that I’m just always moving from one little task to the next. I’ll putter in the kitchen, then fix something that’s been crooked, then arrange a drawer, then say yes to another volunteer thing before I’ve even thought it through. My neighbor used to say, “Sit down before you wear a path in the floor,” and lately I think she’d be right.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s avoidance exactly. What brings me in is more that for the past year or so, I can’t seem to just sit still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities mainly because they help keep your mind active?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but I wouldn’t say “mainly” in a deliberate way. It’s not as if I wake up and think, I must keep my mind active today.\n\nIt’s more that I just seem to keep going—puttering around, fixing little things, arranging this or that, and then someone asks about a volunteer job and I say yes before I’ve really sat with it. My mother was like that too, always had a dish towel in her hand, though she at least seemed to finish one thing before starting another.\n\nBut yes, what brings me in is that for the past year or so, I’ve had such a hard time just sitting down and being still, even at 87.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel bored or unfulfilled if you weren't constantly doing these activities?\n\nPatient: I suppose I might feel a bit restless, more than bored exactly. “Unfulfilled” sounds a little grand for it, doesn’t it? I’m not climbing mountains—I’m straightening the pantry or fixing a loose handle or saying yes to help with some little volunteer thing.\n\nBut yes, if I’m not doing something, I do seem to get up and find something to do. I’ll sit down with tea, and then notice the cushions are crooked, and then I’m in the other room sorting something. My niece says I’m like one of those wind-up toys—she had one as a child, a little tin chicken, I think.\n\nWhat brings me in is that for the past year or so I just feel like I’m always puttering, fixing, arranging, or taking on one more thing, and even at 87 I have a hard time simply sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these activities because you would otherwise feel mentally unstimulated?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it as “mentally unstimulated,” exactly. That sounds like I’m doing a crossword to keep the brain sharp—my sister used to do those in ink, which always seemed very confident to me.\n\nFor me it’s more… I just don’t seem to stop. What brings me in is that for the past year or so I feel like I’m always puttering, fixing something, arranging things, or volunteering for one more thing, and even at 87 I have a hard time just sitting down and being still. So maybe it’s stimulation in a way, but it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it for that reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be physically active to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—comfortable is a good word for it, actually. If I sit too long, I start noticing things that need doing. A drawer that doesn’t close quite right, a plant that needs turning toward the window, someone from the church asking for help with a sign-up sheet—oh, I used to be very good with those, though now everyone wants things online, which is a nuisance.\n\nBut yes, for the past year or so I just feel like I’m always up and doing something—puttering, fixing, arranging, or saying yes to one more thing. Even at 87, I have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not like that, no. I’m always puttering or arranging or signing myself up for one more thing. Even at 87, I seem to have a hard time just sitting down and being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing all these activities to avoid thinking about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not avoiding something in particular. Perfect? No, I don’t think that’s quite it. I mean, I like things decent, you know—my mother used to say a room should look “ready for company,” though heaven knows company doesn’t announce itself much anymore.

But I don’t know that I’m trying to make things perfect. Sometimes I just notice something and then I’m doing it before I’ve really decided to. A crooked stack of papers, a little thing out of place… but not because it has to be flawless, exactly. It’s more just… I don’t leave it alone.
